40. as40 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung 5 (Clarify the picture)

A counselor has been using the same standardized tool for all clients regardless of their cultural background. What is the primary psychometric concern?
A. ✔ +3 KEY
Cultural bias — the normative sample may not represent the client’s group, producing invalid comparisons
An instrument normed on an unrepresentative population compares the client's scores against the wrong reference.
B. 0 near-miss
Construct validity — the tool may be measuring somewhat different constructs across different cultural groups
Construct validity is a real concern, but the primary issue in cross-cultural use is the normative reference group.
Common mistake: Right psychometric concept, wrong emphasis for the specific cross-cultural application asked about.
C. -1 novice error
Test-retest reliability — different clients will score somewhat differently across administrations of the tool
Test-retest reliability is a property of the instrument itself, not a cross-cultural application issue.
Common mistake: Applying an instrument-property framework to a cross-cultural application question — a category mismatch.
D. -2 harmful
Inter-rater reliability — different counselors will score the same tool differently across cultural client groups
Inter-rater reliability concerns examiner agreement; it does not address cultural applicability of the norms.
Common mistake: Confusing an examiner-agreement question with a norms-representativeness question — a concept swap.
Rule: Standardized tools across cultural groups → primary concern is cultural bias in the normative sample; check whether the client’s group is represented.
Reference: Cultural bias in assessment

41. as41 · domain: intake · category: Assessment skills · rung 5 (Clarify the picture)

A counselor completes an intake and notes three possible diagnoses that could explain the client's presentation. What is the correct next step?
A. ✔ +3 KEY
Differential diagnosis — use added history, screening tools, and collateral to rule each option in or out today
Differential diagnosis is the systematic process of distinguishing between competing diagnostic possibilities.
B. 0 near-miss
Pick the most common of the three possible diagnoses as a starting point and revise later if progress stalls
Frequency does not determine accuracy; each candidate diagnosis needs to be evaluated against the evidence.
Common mistake: Substituting a base-rate heuristic for the systematic differential-diagnosis framework at intake.
C. -1 novice error
List all three possible diagnoses at once and begin treating each of them simultaneously with different modalities
Multiple untested diagnoses driving simultaneous treatment produces disorganized and ineffective care.
Common mistake: Skipping the differential step and treating three hypotheses at once — the diagnostic framework is dropped.
D. -2 harmful
Refer the client to a psychiatrist because multiple candidate diagnoses seem to exceed counselor scope
Differential diagnosis is within counselor scope; refer for medical evaluation, not to avoid the diagnostic work.
Common mistake: Using a scope claim to avoid diagnostic work — a scope framework misapplied to sidestep the task.
Rule: Multiple possible diagnoses → conduct differential diagnosis; evaluate each against history, screening tools, and collateral — do not default to frequency or refer to avoid the work.
Reference: Differential diagnosis process

Core Counseling Attributes

Items: 30 · Profile: recall · Served at: /core-attributes-quiz.html
Scoring: dichotomous. Correct = key (✔), all others wrong.

1. ca01 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A client discloses she has been having an affair. The counselor says, "Tell me more about what that experience has been like for you." This BEST demonstrates:
A.
Congruence — the counselor is authentically expressing curiosity about the affair disclosure
Congruence is inner-outer alignment; simple inquiry does not display it.
B. ✔
Unconditional positive regard — nonjudgmental acceptance of the client regardless of behavior
Inviting exploration without moral evaluation is the operational marker of UPR.
C.
Advanced empathy — the counselor is reflecting the implied shame and guilt behind her disclosure
Advanced empathy names implicit affect; the response only invites her to speak.
D.
Accurate empathy — the counselor is reflecting the emotional content of what she has just shared
Empathy reflects felt experience; an open invitation is not a reflection.
Rationale: UPR is valuing the client's personhood regardless of behavior. Inviting exploration without implied judgment operationalizes UPR.

2. ca02 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A counselor responds: "You sound exhausted by all of this — like you've been carrying it alone for a long time." The client says, "Exactly. That's exactly it." This exchange BEST illustrates:
A.
Unconditional positive regard — the counselor is signaling nonjudgmental acceptance of the client
UPR is acceptance of personhood; the reflection tracks affect, not worth.
B.
Congruence — the counselor is aligning inner experience with outer expression in the response
Congruence is genuineness; nothing here reveals the counselor's own experience.
C. ✔
Accurate empathic understanding successfully communicated to the client in a way that landed
The client's 'Exactly' confirms empathy was tracked AND communicated.
D.
Advanced empathy — the counselor is reflecting a meaning the client has not yet put into words
Advanced empathy names the unspoken; exhaustion had already been implied.
Rationale: Accurate empathy is felt tracking PLUS successful communication; the client's confirmation shows both landed.

3. ca03 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

During a session, a counselor notices they feel genuinely bored and distracted. Acting on congruence, the MOST appropriate response is:
A.
Continue the session without any disclosure because showing boredom would violate the UPR condition
Congruence requires disclosure when therapeutically relevant, not suppression.
B. ✔
Disclose the experience when relevant: "I notice my attention drifting; I want to stay with you here."
Brief, relevant disclosure returned to the client's process operationalizes congruence.
C.
End the session early and reschedule the appointment for a time when the counselor is more focused
Terminating avoids the transaction; congruence is about naming, not withdrawing.
D.
Ask the client to slow their pace so the counselor can follow the material more closely in session
Locating the problem in the client masks the counselor's own experience.
Rationale: Congruence means inner experience, outward expression, and words align. Brief, relevant, returned to client.

4. ca04 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

Rogers argued that the three core conditions are "necessary and sufficient" for therapeutic change. This means:
A.
Techniques are helpful additions but are secondary to the relational conditions that produce change
This softens Rogers' claim; he held techniques were not required at all.
B. ✔
No additional therapeutic techniques are required as long as all three conditions are present
"Sufficient" is Rogers' claim that the conditions alone can produce change.
C.
All three of the conditions must be present at the same time in every moment of every session
Rogers required consistency in the relationship, not moment-by-moment perfection.
D.
Skilled counselors need the three conditions plus at least one evidence-based technique to work
This reverses Rogers' claim by making a technique necessary.
Rationale: The conditions are necessary (required) AND sufficient (nothing else needed) — Rogers' radical relational claim.

5. ca05 · domain: core · topic: Rogers' Core Conditions

A counselor says, "I think you should reconsider leaving that relationship — you've worked hard on it." This response MOST directly violates:
A.
Congruence — the counselor is not being genuine or authentic about their own inner state right now
The response may be genuine; the flaw is evaluative direction, not falseness.
B.
Empathy — the counselor has not first reflected the feelings that the client had just expressed
Failure to reflect is present but secondary; the primary violation is evaluative.
C. ✔
Unconditional positive regard — the counselor is directing and evaluating the client's own choices
Prescribing a choice conveys conditional acceptance based on the client's decision.
D.
The therapeutic frame — advice-giving is prohibited across nearly every counseling modality
Directive input is used in many modalities; the Rogerian violation is UPR.
Rationale: UPR is violated when the counselor evaluates or directs the client's choices rather than accepting the person.

6. ca06 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

A client says, "I don't understand why we're doing all this breathing stuff — I came here because my marriage is falling apart." This represents a rupture in:
A.
Bond — the client does not feel emotionally safe with the counselor sitting across from them
No safety concern is stated; the complaint targets methods, not the relationship.
B.
Goals — the client and the counselor do not agree about what therapy is supposed to be for
The client accepts the marriage as the goal; disagreement is with the method.
C. ✔
Tasks — the client is questioning whether the current session methods connect to their own goals
A task rupture is exactly this: shared goal, disputed means.
D.
Bond and tasks equally — safety concerns and questions about methods are both being expressed
The stem raises method only; treating both as equal misreads the rupture.
Rationale: Task rupture: goal is intact (save the marriage) but the client cannot see how the method serves it.

7. ca07 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

A client attends sessions but repeatedly says things like "I guess if you think that's helpful" and "sure, whatever you say." This pattern MOST likely signals:
A.
Good therapeutic compliance — the client is genuinely engaged in the direction set by the counselor
Passive assent is not engagement; it hides disagreement from the counselor.
B. ✔
A goal rupture — the client and the counselor are not sharing the same treatment objectives
Compliance without buy-in is the classic goal-rupture presentation.
C.
A bond rupture — the client no longer feels emotionally safe with the counselor in session
The client is still attending and speaking; bond signals differ (withdrawal).
D.
A task rupture — the client is questioning the counselor's methods, not their overall aims
Task ruptures voice method complaints; passive assent points to goals.
Rationale: Passive compliance flags a goal rupture: the client is going along without endorsing the aim.

8. ca08 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

A client who was abused as a child says tearfully, "I just don't think I can talk about what happened — it doesn't feel safe." The FIRST clinical priority is:
A.
Introduce EMDR right away as a nonverbal alternative to discussing the trauma out loud in session
Starting an exposure-based method before safety is established repeats the harm.
B. ✔
Address the bond component: create conditions of felt safety before any trauma processing occurs
"Doesn't feel safe" names a bond issue; bond is foundational to trauma work.
C.
Renegotiate the treatment plan tasks to include stabilization work ahead of trauma processing
Right principle, wrong level: bond precedes both task and goal renegotiation.
D.
Refer the client to an outside trauma specialist who can provide the felt safety they are needing
Referral abandons a workable rupture; bond can be repaired in-session.
Rationale: "Doesn't feel safe" is a bond signal. Bond is foundational — everything else waits for it.

9. ca09 · domain: core · topic: Bordin's Working Alliance

Research on the therapeutic alliance consistently shows that it is:
A.
Less predictive of client outcome than the specific evidence-based technique used in a modality
Meta-analyses find the reverse: alliance predicts more variance than technique.
B. ✔
The strongest common-factor predictor of treatment outcome across a wide range of modalities
Wampold and Lambert converge on the alliance as the top common factor.
C.
Important only in humanistic or person-centered approaches, not the more directive modalities
Alliance effects are found in CBT, psychodynamic, and other modalities.
D.
Determined mostly by pre-existing client factors rather than by observable counselor behavior
Counselor behavior contributes; the alliance is a two-person process.
Rationale: Alliance is the strongest common-factor predictor of outcome across modalities (Wampold, Lambert).

10. ca10 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client who usually engages openly arrives, gives one-word answers, avoids eye contact, and does not elaborate. The counselor's BEST response is:
A.
Continue the session agenda as planned because the client may just be having a difficult day today
Proceeding as if nothing changed leaves the withdrawal rupture unaddressed.
B.
Confront the withdrawal by asking directly, "What's wrong today? You seem completely different now."
The demand for an answer intensifies withdrawal instead of inviting engagement.
C. ✔
Tentatively name the shift: "I notice you seem quieter than usual today — I want to check in."
Naming the observation and offering warmth is the metacommunication repair move.
D.
Interpret the behavior as resistance and say, "It seems like you're not ready to work today."
Labeling withdrawal as resistance is blaming and hardens the rupture.
Rationale: Withdrawal ruptures need tentative metacommunication — name the shift, invite exploration.

11. ca11 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client says directly, "I feel like you're not really listening to me — you keep steering us back to CBT when I just want to be heard." This is BEST classified as:
A.
A withdrawal rupture — the client is pulling back and disengaging from the session in front of you
The client is actively confronting, not withdrawing.
B. ✔
A confrontation rupture — the client is directly challenging the counselor's approach in the session
Direct challenge to the counselor is the definition of a confrontation rupture.
C.
A termination signal that would require an explicit discussion of ending the counseling arrangement
The client is engaged and asking for change, not signaling termination.
D.
Transference — the client is displacing frustration from another relationship onto the counselor
A concrete complaint about a real behavior is not a transference reading.
Rationale: Confrontation ruptures involve direct disagreement with or challenge to the counselor.

12. ca12 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

Research on therapeutic rupture and repair (Safran & Muran) found that:
A.
Ruptures should be prevented at all costs so as to maintain a positive therapeutic climate over time
Ruptures are inevitable; the outcome depends on repair, not prevention.
B. ✔
Successfully repaired ruptures can strengthen the alliance and improve counseling outcomes overall
Safran & Muran show repaired ruptures deepen alliance and improve outcome.
C.
A single unrepaired rupture reliably predicts that the client will drop out of treatment shortly
One rupture does not reliably cause dropout; repair rate matters more.
D.
Effective repair mostly requires interpreting the rupture as transference from the past instead
Metacommunication in the here-and-now, not transference talk, is the model.
Rationale: Repaired ruptures strengthen the alliance and improve outcomes (Safran & Muran).

13. ca13 · domain: core · topic: Rupture & Repair

A client says, "I felt hurt when you cut me off last session." The MOST effective repair response is:
A.
"That's not what happened — I was trying to redirect us to something more clinically productive."
Correcting the client denies their experience and prevents repair.
B.
"Let's put that aside so we can focus on what you actually came in to work on today in session."
Deferring the rupture blocks metacommunication and models avoidance.
C. ✔
"I appreciate you telling me. Can you say more about how that landed? I want to understand it."
Acknowledge, invite exploration, and stay with it — the Safran & Muran move.
D.
"I'm sorry, that was completely wrong of me. I'll be more mindful and I will make sure to fix it."
Premature apology closes the exploration and prioritizes counselor comfort.
Rationale: Repair sequence: acknowledge, explore what happened for the client, stay with it before repairing.

14. ca14 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A new client arrives for their first session and immediately says, "So what should I work on?" The counselor's FIRST priority is:
A.
Give the client several evidence-based options and encourage them to pick the one that fits best
Choosing a modality before alliance and goals is a stage error.
B. ✔
Establish rapport and collaboratively explore what brought them in and what they are hoping for
Stage 1 is alliance and shared understanding before any goal-setting.
C.
Start a formal assessment battery so the counselor can rule in or out major clinical diagnoses
Structured assessment belongs to stage 2, after alliance is established.
D.
Provide psychoeducation about the counseling process and set homework for the second session
Psychoeducation is fine but not the priority before the alliance is formed.
Rationale: Stage 1 (Egan / Ivey): rapport and shared story precede assessment, goals, and intervention.

15. ca15 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A client and counselor have completed a thorough assessment and share a working understanding of the problem. The NEXT stage is:
A.
Continue exploration of the presenting problem to gather still more information from the client
Further exploration delays goal-setting once shared understanding exists.
B.
Move directly into confrontation of the client's defenses about the problem they have described
Confrontation of defenses is not a standard stage of the counseling model.
C. ✔
Set concrete, measurable, collaborative goals for what the client wants to change in counseling
After the shared story, stage 2 collaboratively sets change-focused goals.
D.
Terminate services because the client can now work on the problem independently outside sessions
Termination follows goal attainment, not the completion of assessment.
Rationale: Model sequence: alliance/story -> goal setting -> intervention -> maintenance/termination.

16. ca16 · domain: counseling · topic: Stages of Counseling

A client says, "I feel so much better — I don't know if I need to come back." The counselor's BEST response is:
A.
Agree to terminate today and wish them well since improvement is the goal of the entire process
Abrupt termination skips relapse planning and consolidation of gains.
B. ✔
Explore the improvement and collaboratively plan a stepped-down termination with relapse prevention
Stage 4 termination is stepped, planned, and includes relapse prevention.
C.
Warn the client that stopping now will cause a relapse and firmly recommend continuing sessions
Alarmist warnings undermine autonomy and the alliance.
D.
Interpret the wish to stop as a rupture, avoidance, or resistance that requires deeper exploration
Reading normal termination readiness as resistance pathologizes progress.
Rationale: Termination is a stage: explore the improvement, plan a stepped exit, include relapse prevention.

17. ca17 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

A client from a collectivist cultural background says, "I can't decide about the job without talking to my family first." The BEST response is:
A.
Encourage the client to prioritize their own individual autonomy over the family's preferences here
Imposing individualism ignores culturally embedded decision-making.
B. ✔
Explore how the family conversations fit the client's own decision-making process and identity
Cultural humility is exploring the client's frame, not correcting it.
C.
Interpret the deference to family as enmeshment and set a clearer boundary with them in session
Labeling collectivism "enmeshment" is a Western pathologizing frame.
D.
Refer the client to a counselor from the same culture on the grounds that scope has been exceeded
Reflexive referral is not required and can feel like abandonment.
Rationale: Cultural humility explores the client's frame without imposing an individualist standard.

18. ca18 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

The MSJCC (Multicultural and Social Justice Counseling Competencies) emphasize which sequence?
A. ✔
Attitudes and beliefs first, then knowledge, then skills, then action — a developmental sequence
The MSJCC domains build in this order across all counselor–client interactions.
B.
Techniques first, followed by knowledge of the client's culture, and only then counselor awareness
This reverses the MSJCC ordering, which begins with awareness.
C.
Complete cultural competence first, then skills, and finally social-justice advocacy work with clients
MSJCC treats advocacy as concurrent, not a later stage after competence.
D.
Client demographics first, then interventions matched to the demographic profile, and then advocacy
Demographics are not the entry point; counselor awareness is.
Rationale: MSJCC ordering: attitudes/beliefs -> knowledge -> skills -> action, across four domains.

19. ca19 · domain: core · topic: Cultural Responsiveness

A White counselor notices they feel uncomfortable when a Black client discusses experiences of racism. The BEST first step is:
A.
Change the topic to prevent the discomfort from spilling into the therapeutic relationship over time
Avoidance silences the client and reinforces the counselor's discomfort.
B. ✔
Explore the discomfort in supervision or consultation without burdening the client with it in session
Cultural humility requires the counselor's own work; the client is not the classroom.
C.
Disclose the discomfort in session to model transparency and openness with the client immediately
Well-meant disclosure here centers the counselor's reaction over the client's.
D.
Refer the client to a counselor of color to avoid causing further harm in the current relationship
Reflexive referral offloads the counselor's work onto the client.
Rationale: Counselor discomfort about race is worked in supervision — not offloaded to the client.

20. ca20 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor asks a client to complete a thought record examining automatic thoughts, evidence for and against, and a balanced alternative view. This is:
A.
Psychodynamic therapy — interpretation of the underlying conflicts driving the client's current distress
Thought records are Beckian CBT, not psychodynamic interpretation.
B. ✔
Cognitive-behavioral therapy — examining automatic thoughts, evidence, and balanced alternatives
Thought records are the signature CBT technique for restructuring cognitions.
C.
Person-centered therapy — reflecting the client's felt experience without directive intervention here
Person-centered work does not use structured cognitive worksheets.
D.
Solution-focused therapy — building exceptions and scaling toward the preferred future outcomes
SFBT uses exceptions and scales, not thought records.
Rationale: Thought records = Beckian CBT: identify automatic thoughts, weigh evidence, generate a balanced view.

21. ca21 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor helps a client identify their family birth order, sibling constellation, and early recollections. This is:
A. ✔
Adlerian (individual psychology) — using lifestyle assessment, family constellation, early recollections
Adler's hallmark tools: birth order, constellation, and early recollections.
B.
Bowenian family systems therapy — genogram construction and analysis of multigenerational patterns
Genograms and differentiation are Bowen; Adler owns the earlier tools listed.
C.
Jungian analytical therapy — exploring the archetypes and the deeper collective unconscious material
Jungian work centers archetypes and the collective unconscious, not birth order.
D.
Object-relations therapy — the exploration of internalized representations of parents and caregivers
Object relations focuses on internalized objects, not constellation data.
Rationale: Adler = lifestyle assessment, family constellation, birth order, early recollections.

22. ca22 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor uses "the miracle question" and asks the client to scale progress from 1-10. This is:
A.
Narrative therapy — externalizing the problem so it becomes something the client can act against in life
Externalizing is White & Epston narrative; miracles/scales are SFBT.
B. ✔
Solution-focused brief therapy — using the miracle question and scaling questions to build progress
Miracle question and scaling questions are the signature SFBT tools.
C.
Motivational interviewing — evoking the client's own change talk about the target behavior over time
MI evokes change talk with OARS; miracle/scaling are not MI signatures.
D.
Reality therapy (choice theory) — addressing the client's five basic needs and current choices
Reality therapy uses WDEP and choice theory, not miracle questions.
Rationale: de Shazer / Berg SFBT: miracle question, exceptions, scaling toward the preferred future.

23. ca23 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor asks a client to accept their thoughts as thoughts (not facts) and commit to values-based action. This is:
A.
Traditional cognitive therapy — disputing the content of the automatic thoughts and replacing them
ACT changes relationship with thoughts; content-disputing is Beck / Ellis.
B.
Dialectical behavior therapy — balancing acceptance with change work through the DBT skill modules
DBT is dialectical but not organized around psychological flexibility.
C. ✔
Acceptance and commitment therapy — building psychological flexibility through defusion and values
Defusion + values-guided action is Hayes' ACT model of flexibility.
D.
Reality therapy — helping the client take responsibility for their choices and current commitments
Reality therapy uses WDEP; ACT uses defusion and values.
Rationale: ACT builds psychological flexibility: acceptance, defusion, present moment, values, committed action.

24. ca24 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor confronts a client's "musts" and "shoulds" and asks them to dispute these rigid demands actively. This is:
A.
Beckian cognitive therapy — a Socratic examination of the evidence for automatic thoughts held today
Beck uses Socratic inquiry; Ellis directly disputes "musts" and "shoulds."
B. ✔
Rational emotive behavior therapy — disputing the client's irrational beliefs, demands, and "musts"
Ellis' REBT names and disputes musturbation and rigid demands.
C.
Motivational interviewing — evoking the client's own reasons for change through open-ended reflection
MI is nondirective evocation; direct disputation is not MI-consistent.
D.
Person-centered therapy — providing accurate reflections without any directive challenge in session
Person-centered work does not confront or dispute the client's beliefs.
Rationale: REBT (Ellis): identify absolute demands (must, should, ought) and dispute them directly.

25. ca25 · domain: core · topic: Theory Identification

A counselor helps a client construct a preferred alternative story by asking about unique outcomes and exceptions. This is:
A.
Solution-focused brief therapy — scaling and exception questions to build small changes over sessions
SFBT uses exceptions too, but narrative owns "unique outcomes" and re-storying.
B. ✔
Narrative therapy — externalizing the problem and identifying unique outcomes to re-author the story
White & Epston: externalize, find unique outcomes, thicken a preferred story.
C.
Existential therapy — addressing the givens of existence such as freedom, isolation, and mortality
Existential work targets the givens, not story re-authoring.
D.
Adlerian therapy — exploring the client's lifestyle patterns and their subjective view of the past
Adler uses lifestyle, not unique outcomes and re-storying.
Rationale: Narrative therapy (White & Epston): externalize the problem, find unique outcomes, thicken the alternative story.

26. ca26 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "I don't know why I even bother trying anymore." The counselor responds, "You sound worn down." This is:
A.
A summary — the counselor is pulling together the major themes covered so far in the session material
A summary integrates several statements; this reflects a single feeling.
B. ✔
A reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the emotional experience behind what the client said
Naming the client's emotion IS a reflection of feeling.
C.
A paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
A paraphrase mirrors the content, not the underlying emotion.
D.
An interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper meaning that the client has not yet articulated
"Worn down" mirrors what was implied; it does not add a new meaning.
Rationale: Reflection of feeling names the emotion; paraphrase mirrors content; interpretation adds new meaning.

27. ca27 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "My boss said my work isn't up to standard." The counselor: "Your boss criticized your work." This is:
A. ✔
Paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
Restating the factual content in the counselor's own words is a paraphrase.
B.
Reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the underlying emotional experience of the client
No feeling is named; only the content is restated.
C.
Summary — the counselor is pulling together the major themes covered so far in the client's session
A summary integrates multiple statements, not one.
D.
Confrontation — the counselor is pointing out an inconsistency in the client's described experience
No discrepancy is pointed out; this is a straight content mirror.
Rationale: Paraphrase = restating factual content; reflection of feeling = naming affect.

28. ca28 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client says, "I love my job, but I dread going to work every morning." The counselor: "You say you love it, and you dread it." This is:
A.
Paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
The counselor pointed out contradictory statements, not merely restated content.
B.
Reflection of feeling — the counselor is naming the emotional experience behind what the client said
No feeling label is used; the counselor highlighted a discrepancy.
C. ✔
Confrontation — the counselor is naming a discrepancy or mixed message in the client's statements
Naming a "you say X and Y" contradiction is Ivey-style confrontation.
D.
Interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper explanation of the meaning of the discrepancy
The counselor named the discrepancy without offering a new explanatory frame.
Rationale: Confrontation (Ivey) names a discrepancy without judgment: "on one hand X, on the other Y."

29. ca29 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A client speaks for 10 minutes about a difficult week. The counselor pauses and offers a brief summary of the themes. This BEST reflects:
A.
A reflection of feeling that names the primary emotion running through the client's difficult week
Feeling reflections name one affect; a summary integrates themes.
B. ✔
A summary that integrates the major themes the client raised across the entire ten-minute segment
Integrating several statements into themes is a summary.
C.
An interpretation that offers a deeper unconscious meaning that the client has not yet articulated
A theme summary is not an interpretation of unconscious material.
D.
Immediacy — the counselor is naming what is happening between them in the here-and-now interaction
Immediacy speaks to the counselor–client interaction, not week themes.
Rationale: Summaries integrate major themes across a stretch of session content, orienting both parties.

30. ca30 · domain: counseling · topic: Microskills

A counselor says, "I notice we've been going back and forth about your job, but you keep pulling us into work when I ask about home." This is:
A.
A summary — the counselor is integrating major themes discussed across the previous session material
A summary integrates themes; this names an in-session pattern.
B.
A paraphrase — the counselor is restating the informational content of what the client just described
A paraphrase mirrors content; immediacy names the process.
C. ✔
Immediacy — the counselor is naming what is happening between them in the here-and-now interaction
Naming what is happening between counselor and client right now is immediacy.
D.
Interpretation — the counselor is offering a deeper meaning of the pattern in the client's life history
Interpretation adds new meaning; immediacy stays with the observable process.
Rationale: Immediacy speaks to the here-and-now counselor–client relationship in the session.

Catastrophizing

Mind reading

"Teach clients new coping skills through instruction at intak…" is not what the item tests — Socratic questioning is guided discovery — using targeted questions to help clients evaluate the accuracy and utility of their thoughts rather than

'Privilege' is the client's separate legal right in court contexts.

C.

"It provides the client with a sense of control over their en…" is not what the item tests — Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited.

Rationale: Means restriction works because suicidal crises are typically acute and time-limited. Most people who survive an attempt do not go on to die by suicide. C.
Store records exclusively on local hardware with no cloud storage as usually described
"Store records exclusively on local hardware with no cloud st…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and admi
D.
Conduct annual third-party security audits of all systems as clinicians typically apply it
"Conduct annual third-party security audits of all systems as…" is not what the item tests — HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and admi
Rationale: HIPAA Security Rule requires "reasonable and appropriate" safeguards — physical (locked storage), technical (encryption, access controls), and administrative (policies, training). The standard is reasonable safeguards, not specific technology mandates. Cloud storage is permissible with a Business Associate Agreement.

249. asx009 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: medium

Reliability is necessary but not sufficient for validity — a measure can be reliably measuring the wrong thing.

251. asx011 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: hard

A test with high sensitivity but low specificity means:
A. ✔
It correctly identifies true positives but also produces many false positives
Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).
B.
It correctly identifies true negatives but misses many true positives as used
"It correctly identifies true negatives but misses many true …" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).
C.
It produces consistent results but does not measure the target construct here
"It produces consistent results but does not measure the targ…" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).
D.
It is appropriate for high-stakes diagnostic decisions during a routine session
"It is appropriate for high-stakes diagnostic decisions durin…" is not what the item tests — Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate).
Rationale: Sensitivity = ability to correctly identify those WITH the condition (true positive rate). High sensitivity = few false negatives. Specificity = ability to correctly identify those WITHOUT the condition. Low specificity = many false positives. High sensitivity/low specificity = good for screening (don't miss cases) but produces overidentification.

252. asx012 · domain: assessment · subdomain: Psychometrics · difficulty: medium

B. ✔
Comparing it to the performance of a relevant normative comparison group
Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").
C.
Evaluating it against the client's own previous performance by counselors
"Evaluating it against the client's own previous performance …" is not what the item tests — Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40").
D.

Rationale: Norm-referenced assessment locates a score in relation to a standardization sample (e.g., "above the 85th percentile for adults aged 30–40"). Criterion-referenced assessment evaluates performance against an absolute standard (e.g., "met the cutoff for clinical depression"). Most standardized personality and cognitive tests are norm-referenced.

Rationale: Static risk factors (prior attempts, history of trauma, demographic factors) are fixed and establish baseline risk. Dynamic risk factors (current hopelessness, substance use, access to means, social isolation) are modifiable and more predictive of imminent risk. 277. dx017 · domain: diagnosis · subdomain: MalingeringVsFactitious · difficulty: hard

In factitious disorder, symptoms are not intentionally produced as the reference material frames it at intake as the reference material frames it

B. ✔
Malingering involves external incentives (financial, legal); factitious disorder is motivated by assuming the sick role without external gain
Both involve intentional symptom production.
C.

Rationale: Both involve intentional symptom production. Distinction is motivation: Malingering = external incentives (disability payments, avoiding legal consequences, obtaining medications). "Determined by the insurance company’s authorized number of s…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.
B. ✔
Defined at the outset as observable, measurable indicators that goals have been achieved
Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.
C.

"Left open-ended to allow flexibility as treatment progresses…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.
D.
Set after the client achieves at least 80% symptom reduction at the level the item tests
"Set after the client achieves at least 80% symptom reduction…" is not what the item tests — Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target.
Rationale: Discharge criteria defined at the outset give both client and counselor a shared target. They typically include symptom thresholds (e.g., PHQ-9 < 5), functional indicators (return to work, rebuilt relationships), and skill mastery (client applies techniques independently). Insurance authorization is administrative, not a clinical criterion.

297. tp017 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: DischargeCriteria · difficulty: medium

A client with panic disorder has had no panic attacks for 6 weeks and reports confidence managing symptoms independently. The BEST treatment plan decision is:
A.
Terminate immediately to prevent the development of therapeutic dependency during a routine session
"Terminate immediately to prevent the development of therapeu…" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.
B.
Continue at the same frequency indefinitely to prevent any relapse as clinicians typically apply it
"Continue at the same frequency indefinitely to prevent any r…" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.
C. ✔
Begin tapering sessions, review relapse prevention, and establish a plan for returning if needed
When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.
D.
Switch to open-ended maintenance therapy focused on general wellbeing at the level the item tests
"Switch to open-ended maintenance therapy focused on general …" is not what the item tests — When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end.
Rationale: When discharge criteria are met: taper rather than abruptly end. Moving from weekly to biweekly to monthly builds confidence in independent functioning. Relapse prevention — identifying early warning signs, reviewing coping strategies, clarifying when to return — is required for anxiety disorder termination.

300. tp020 · domain: treatment_planning · subdomain: StepDownPlanning · difficulty: medium

A client is being discharged from inpatient psychiatric hospitalization. The MOST critical component of the step-down plan is:
A.
A written summary of the inpatient treatment provided to the client at the level the item tests
"A written summary of the inpatient treatment provided to the…" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.
B. ✔
A confirmed outpatient appointment within 7 days of discharge, with crisis resources identified
The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.
C.
A 30-day medication supply with written administration instructions at the level the item tests
"A 30-day medication supply with written administration instr…" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.
D.
The client’s verbal agreement to contact the inpatient unit if symptoms worsen during counseling
"The client’s verbal agreement to contact the inpatient unit …" is not what the item tests — The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission.
Rationale: The post-discharge period is the highest-risk window for suicide and psychiatric readmission. A confirmed follow-up appointment within 7 days (ideally 24–72 hours) is the single strongest predictor of successful transition. Without a scheduled appointment, the step-down plan is clinically incomplete regardless of other components.
